<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the modern country since 1990. For the former country from 1975 to 1990, see <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Mozambique" title="People&#39;s Republic of Mozambique">People's Republic of Mozambique</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the country in Africa. <p><b>Mozambique</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˌ/: secondary stress follows">ˌ</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span><span title="&#39;z&#39; in &#39;zoom&#39;">z</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/iː/: &#39;ee&#39; in &#39;fleece&#39;">iː</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span></span>/</a></span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a>: <i lang="pt">Moçambique</i>, <span style="font-size:85%;">pronounced&#160;</span><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="pt-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese" title="Help:IPA/Portuguese">[musɐ̃ˈbikɨ]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Chichewa_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Chichewa language">Chichewa</a>: <i lang="ny">Mozambiki</i>; <a href="/wiki/Swahili_language" title="Swahili language">Swahili</a>: <i lang="sw">Msumbiji</i>; <a href="/wiki/Tsonga_language" title="Tsonga language">Tsonga</a>: <i lang="ts">Muzambhiki</i>), officially the <b>Republic of Mozambique</b> (<span title="Portuguese-language text"><i lang="pt">República de Moçambique</i></span>, <span style="font-size:85%;">pronounced&#160;</span><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="pt-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese" title="Help:IPA/Portuguese">[ʁɛˈpuβlikɐ ðɨ musɐ̃ˈbikɨ]</a></span>), is a country located in southeastern <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a> bordered by the Indian Ocean to the east, <a href="/wiki/Tanzania" title="Tanzania">Tanzania</a> to the north, <a href="/wiki/Malawi" title="Malawi">Malawi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Zambia" title="Zambia">Zambia</a> to the northwest, <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe">Zimbabwe</a> to the west, and <a href="/wiki/Eswatini" title="Eswatini">Eswatini</a> and <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a> to the southwest. The <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a> is separated from the <a href="/wiki/Comoros" title="Comoros">Comoros</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mayotte" title="Mayotte">Mayotte</a> and <a href="/wiki/Madagascar" title="Madagascar">Madagascar</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Mozambique_Channel" title="Mozambique Channel">Mozambique Channel</a> to the east. The capital and largest city is <a href="/wiki/Maputo" title="Maputo">Maputo</a>.
</p><p>Northern Mozambique lies within the <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoon</a> <a href="/wiki/Trade_winds" title="Trade winds">trade winds</a> of the Indian Ocean and is frequently affected by disruptive weather. Between the 7th and 11th centuries, a series of Swahili port towns developed on that area, which contributed to the development of a distinct <a href="/wiki/Swahili_culture" title="Swahili culture">Swahili culture</a> and dialect. In the late medieval period, these towns were frequented by traders from <a href="/wiki/Somalia" title="Somalia">Somalia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arabian_Peninsula" title="Arabian Peninsula">Arabia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Persia</a>, and India.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The voyage of <a href="/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama" title="Vasco da Gama">Vasco da Gama</a> in 1498 marked the arrival of the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese</a>, who began a gradual process of colonisation and settlement in 1505. After over four centuries of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Mozambique" title="Portuguese Mozambique">Portuguese rule</a>, Mozambique <a href="/wiki/Mozambican_War_of_Independence" title="Mozambican War of Independence">gained independence</a> in 1975, becoming the <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Mozambique" title="People&#39;s Republic of Mozambique">People's Republic of Mozambique</a> shortly thereafter. After only two years of independence, the country descended into an intense and protracted <a href="/wiki/Mozambican_Civil_War" title="Mozambican Civil War">civil war lasting from 1977 to 1992</a>. In 1994, Mozambique held its first multiparty elections and has since remained a relatively stable <a href="/wiki/Presidential_system" title="Presidential system">presidential republic</a>, although it still faces a <a href="/wiki/Insurgency_in_Cabo_Delgado" title="Insurgency in Cabo Delgado">low-intensity insurgency</a> distinctively in the farthermost regions from the southern capital and where Islam is dominant.
</p><p>Mozambique is endowed with rich and extensive natural resources, notwithstanding the country's economy is based chiefly on fishery—substantially <a href="/wiki/Mollusca" title="Mollusca">molluscs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Crustacean" title="Crustacean">crustaceans</a> and <a href="/wiki/Echinoderm" title="Echinoderm">echinoderms</a>—and agriculture with a growing industry of food and beverages, chemical manufacturing, aluminium and oil. The tourism sector is expanding. South Africa remains Mozambique's main trading partner, preserving a close relationship with <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> with a perspective on other European markets.
</p><p>Since 2001, Mozambique's GDP growth has been thriving, but the nation is still one of the poorest and most <a href="/wiki/Developing_country" title="Developing country">underdeveloped countries</a> in the world,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> ranking low in <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita" title="List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita">GDP per capita</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index" title="List of countries by Human Development Index">human development</a>, measures of <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_inequality-adjusted_Human_Development_Index" title="List of countries by inequality-adjusted Human Development Index">inequality</a> and average <a href="/wiki/Life_expectancy" title="Life expectancy">life expectancy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The country's population of around 30 million, as of 2022 estimates, is composed of overwhelmingly <a href="/wiki/Bantu_peoples" title="Bantu peoples">Bantu peoples</a>. However, the only official language in Mozambique is the colonial language of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a>, which is spoken in urban areas as a first or second language by most, and generally as a <a href="/wiki/Lingua_franca" title="Lingua franca">lingua franca</a> between younger Mozambicans with access to formal education. The most important local languages include <a href="/wiki/Tsonga_language" title="Tsonga language">Tsonga</a>, <a href="/wiki/Makhuwa_language" title="Makhuwa language">Makhuwa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sena_language" title="Sena language">Sena</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chewa_language" title="Chewa language">Chichewa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Swahili_language" title="Swahili language">Swahili</a>. <a href="/wiki/Glottolog" title="Glottolog">Glottolog</a> lists 46 languages spoken in the country,<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> of which one is a signed language (Mozambican Sign Language/<i>Língua de sinais de Moçambique</i>). The largest <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Mozambique" title="Religion in Mozambique">religion in Mozambique</a> is Christianity, with significant minorities following Islam and <a href="/wiki/Traditional_African_religions" title="Traditional African religions">African traditional religions</a>. 
</p><p>Mozambique is a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>, the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of Islamic Cooperation</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Community_of_Portuguese_Language_Countries" title="Community of Portuguese Language Countries">Community of Portuguese Language Countries</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community" title="Southern African Development Community">Southern African Development Community</a>, and is an observer at <a href="/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie" title="Organisation internationale de la Francophonie">La Francophonie</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The country was named Moçambique by the Portuguese after the <a href="/wiki/Island_of_Mozambique" title="Island of Mozambique">Island of Mozambique</a>, derived from <i><a href="/wiki/Mussa_Bin_Bique" title="Mussa Bin Bique">Mussa Bin Bique</a></i> or <i>Musa Al Big</i> or <i>Mossa Al Bique</i> or <i>Mussa Ben Mbiki</i> or <i>Mussa Ibn Malik</i>, an Arab trader who first visited the island and later lived there.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The island-town was the capital of the Portuguese colony until 1898, when it was moved south to Lourenço Marques (now <a href="/wiki/Maputo" title="Maputo">Maputo</a>).
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Mozambique" title="History of Mozambique">History of Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mozambique_(3912235854).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Mozambique_%283912235854%29.jpg/220px-Mozambique_%283912235854%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Mozambique_%283912235854%29.jpg/330px-Mozambique_%283912235854%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Mozambique_%283912235854%29.jpg/440px-Mozambique_%283912235854%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1861" data-file-height="1246" /></a><figcaption>Mozambican <a href="/wiki/Dhow" title="Dhow">dhow</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bantu_migrations">Bantu migrations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Bantu migrations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Bantu_expansion" title="Bantu expansion">Bantu expansion</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Bantu_languages" title="Bantu languages">Bantu</a>-speaking peoples migrated into Mozambique as early as the 4th century BC.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> It is believed between the 1st and 5th centuries AD, waves of migration from the west and north went through the <a href="/wiki/Zambezi" title="Zambezi">Zambezi</a> River valley and then gradually into the plateau and coastal areas of Southern Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> They established agricultural communities or societies based on herding cattle. They brought with them the technology for smelting<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> and smithing iron.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Swahili_Coast">Swahili Coast</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Swahili Coast">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Slaves_ruvuma.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Slaves_ruvuma.jpg/220px-Slaves_ruvuma.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="132" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Slaves_ruvuma.jpg/330px-Slaves_ruvuma.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Slaves_ruvuma.jpg/440px-Slaves_ruvuma.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="602" /></a><figcaption>Arab-Swahili slave traders and their captives on the <a href="/wiki/Ruvuma_River" title="Ruvuma River">Ruvuma River</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>From the late first millennium AD, vast <a href="/wiki/Indian_Ocean_trade" title="Indian Ocean trade">Indian Ocean trade</a> networks extended as far south into Mozambique as evidenced by the ancient port town of <a href="/wiki/Chibuene" title="Chibuene">Chibuene</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sinclair2_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sinclair2-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Beginning in the 9th century, a growing involvement in Indian Ocean trade led to the development of numerous port towns along the entire East African coast, including modern day Mozambique. Largely autonomous, these towns broadly participated in the incipient <a href="/wiki/Swahili_culture" title="Swahili culture">Swahili culture</a>. Islam was often adopted by urban elites, facilitating trade. In Mozambique, <a href="/wiki/Sofala" title="Sofala">Sofala</a>, <a href="/wiki/Angoche" title="Angoche">Angoche</a>, and Mozambique Island were regional powers by the 15th century.
</p><p>The towns traded with merchants from both the African interior and the broader Indian Ocean world. Particularly important were the gold and ivory caravan routes. Inland states like the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Zimbabwe" title="Kingdom of Zimbabwe">Kingdom of Zimbabwe</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Mutapa" title="Kingdom of Mutapa">Kingdom of Mutapa</a> provided the coveted gold and ivory, which were then exchanged up the coast to larger port cities like <a href="/wiki/Kilwa_Kisiwani" title="Kilwa Kisiwani">Kilwa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mombasa" title="Mombasa">Mombasa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Portuguese_Mozambique_.281498.E2.80.931975.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Portuguese_Mozambique_(1498–1975)">Portuguese Mozambique (1498–1975)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Portuguese Mozambique (1498–1975)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Mozambique" title="Portuguese Mozambique">Portuguese Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mozambique_n2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Mozambique_n2.jpg/250px-Mozambique_n2.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Mozambique_n2.jpg/375px-Mozambique_n2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Mozambique_n2.jpg/500px-Mozambique_n2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1472" data-file-height="962" /></a><figcaption>Detail of the <a href="/wiki/Island_of_Mozambique" title="Island of Mozambique">Island of Mozambique</a>, former capital in Northern Mozambique and prominent in the country's history</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg/250px-Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg/375px-Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg/500px-Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3837" data-file-height="2558" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Chapel_of_Nossa_Senhora_de_Baluarte" title="Chapel of Nossa Senhora de Baluarte">Chapel of Nossa Senhora de Baluarte</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg/250px-Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="373" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg/375px-Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg/500px-Fortaleza_de_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o-01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="3872" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Fort_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o" title="Fort São Sebastião">Fort São Sebastião</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Island_of_Mozambique" title="Island of Mozambique">Island of Mozambique</a> after which the country is named, is a small coral island at the mouth of Mossuril Bay on the <a href="/wiki/Nacala" title="Nacala">Nacala</a> coast of northern Mozambique, first explored by Europeans in the late 15th century.
</p><p>When Portuguese explorers reached Mozambique in 1498, Arab-trading settlements had existed along the coast and outlying islands for several centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-gupta_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gupta-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> From about 1500, Portuguese trading posts and forts displaced the Arabic commercial and military hegemony, becoming regular ports of call on the new European sea route to the east,<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EBmoz_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBmoz-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> the first steps in what was to become a process of colonisation.<sup id="cite_ref-EBmoz_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBmoz-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newitt_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newitt-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The voyage of <a href="/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama" title="Vasco da Gama">Vasco da Gama</a> around the <a href="/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope" title="Cape of Good Hope">Cape of Good Hope</a> in 1498 marked the Portuguese entry into trade, politics, and society of the region. The Portuguese gained control of the Island of Mozambique and the port city of Sofala in the early 16th century, and by the 1530s, small groups of Portuguese traders and prospectors seeking gold penetrated the interior regions, where they set up garrisons and trading posts at <a href="/wiki/Sena,_Mozambique" class="mw-redirect" title="Sena, Mozambique">Sena</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tete,_Mozambique" title="Tete, Mozambique">Tete</a> on the Zambezi and tried to gain exclusive control over the gold trade.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the central part of the Mozambique territory, the Portuguese attempted to legitimise and consolidate their trade and settlement positions through the creation of <i><a href="/wiki/Prazo" title="Prazo">prazos</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> These land grants tied emigrants to their settlements, and inland Mozambique was largely left to be administered by <i>prazeiros</i>, the grant holders, while central authorities in Portugal concentrated their direct exercise of power on, in their view, the more important Portuguese possessions in Asia and the Americas.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Isaac2000_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Isaac2000-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Slavery in Mozambique pre-dated European-contact. African rulers and chiefs dealt in enslaved people, first with Arab Muslim traders, who sent the enslaved to Middle East Asia cities and plantations, and later with Portuguese and other European traders. In a continuation of the trade, slaves were supplied by warring local African rulers, who raided enemy tribes and sold their captives to the <i>prazeiros</i>. The authority of the <i>prazeiros</i> was exercised and upheld amongst the local population by armies of these enslaved men, whose members became known as <i><a href="/wiki/Chikunda" title="Chikunda">Chikunda</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Continuing emigration from Portugal occurred at comparatively low levels until late in the nineteenth century, promoting "Africanisation".<sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> While <i>prazos</i> were originally intended to be held solely by Portuguese colonists, through intermarriage and the relative isolation of <i>prazeiros</i> from ongoing Portuguese influences, the <i>prazos</i> became African-Portuguese or African-Indian.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_24-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EBmoz_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBmoz-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg/220px-Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="139" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg/330px-Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg/440px-Lourenco-Marques-pc-c1905.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="380" /></a><figcaption>View of the Central Avenue in Lourenço Marques, now Maputo, ca. 1905</figcaption></figure>
<p>Although Portuguese influence gradually expanded, its power was limited and exercised through individual settlers and officials who were granted extensive autonomy. The Portuguese were able to wrest much of the coastal trade from Arab Muslims between 1500 and 1700, but, with the Arab Muslim seizure of Portugal's key foothold at <a href="/wiki/Fort_Jesus" title="Fort Jesus">Fort Jesus</a> on <a href="/wiki/Mombasa_Island" title="Mombasa Island">Mombasa Island</a> (now in Kenya) in 1698, the pendulum began to swing in the other direction. As a result, investment lagged while <a href="/wiki/Lisbon" title="Lisbon">Lisbon</a> devoted itself to the more lucrative trade with India and the Far East and to the colonisation of Brazil.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Mazrui" title="Mazrui">Mazrui</a> and <a href="/wiki/History_of_Oman" title="History of Oman">Omani Arabs</a> reclaimed much of the Indian Ocean trade, forcing the Portuguese to retreat south. Many <i>prazos</i> had declined by the mid-19th century, but several of them survived. During the 19th century other European powers, particularly the British (<a href="/wiki/British_South_Africa_Company" title="British South Africa Company">British South Africa Company</a>) and the French (Madagascar), became increasingly involved in the trade and politics of the region around the Portuguese East African territories.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg/250px-TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="192" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg/375px-TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg/500px-TT_CMZ-AF-GT_E_2-1_10_81_-_Oficina_de_Tipografia_da_Escola_de_Artes_e_Of%C3%ADcios.jpg 2x" data-file-width="910" data-file-height="700" /></a><figcaption>Portuguese language printing and typesetting class, 1930</figcaption></figure>
<p>By the early 20th century the Portuguese had shifted the administration of much of Mozambique to large private companies, like the <a href="/wiki/Mozambique_Company" title="Mozambique Company">Mozambique Company</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Zambezia_Province" title="Zambezia Province">Zambezia Company</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Niassa_Company" title="Niassa Company">Niassa Company</a>, controlled and financed mostly by British financiers such as <a href="/wiki/Solomon_Joel" title="Solomon Joel">Solomon Joel</a>, which established railroad lines to their neighbouring colonies (South Africa and <a href="/wiki/Rhodesia" title="Rhodesia">Rhodesia</a>). Although slavery had been legally abolished in Mozambique, at the end of the 19th century the chartered companies enacted a forced labour policy and supplied cheap—often forced—African labour to the mines and <a href="/wiki/Plantation" title="Plantation">plantations</a> of the nearby British colonies and South Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The Zambezia Company, the most profitable chartered company, took over several smaller <i>prazeiro</i> holdings and established military outposts to protect its property. The chartered companies built roads and ports to bring their goods to market including a railroad linking present-day Zimbabwe with the Mozambican port of <a href="/wiki/Beira,_Mozambique" title="Beira, Mozambique">Beira</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Cambridge_history_of_Africa_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Cambridge_history_of_Africa-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Third_Portuguese_Empire,_1825-1975_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Third_Portuguese_Empire,_1825-1975-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Due to their unsatisfactory performance and the shift, under the <a href="/wiki/Corporatist" class="mw-redirect" title="Corporatist">corporatist</a> <a href="/wiki/Estado_Novo_(Portugal)" title="Estado Novo (Portugal)">Estado Novo</a> regime of <a href="/wiki/Oliveira_Salazar" class="mw-redirect" title="Oliveira Salazar">Oliveira Salazar</a>, toward a stronger Portuguese control of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese Empire</a>'s economy, the companies' concessions were not renewed when they ran out. This was what happened in 1942 with the Mozambique Company, which, however, continued to operate in the agricultural and commercial sectors as a corporation, and had already happened in 1929 with the termination of the Niassa Company's concession. In 1951, the Portuguese overseas colonies in Africa were rebranded as Overseas Provinces of Portugal.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Cambridge_history_of_Africa_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Cambridge_history_of_Africa-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Third_Portuguese_Empire,_1825-1975_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Third_Portuguese_Empire,_1825-1975-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Mueda_massacre" class="mw-redirect" title="Mueda massacre">Mueda massacre</a> of 16 June 1960, resulted in the death of <a href="/wiki/Makonde_people" title="Makonde people">Makonde</a> protestors, which provoked the struggle of independence from Portuguese rule of Mozambique.
</p>
<h3><span id="Mozambican_War_of_Independence_.281964.E2.80.931975.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mozambican_War_of_Independence_(1964–1975)">Mozambican War of Independence (1964–1975)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Mozambican War of Independence (1964–1975)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mozambican_War_of_Independence" title="Mozambican War of Independence">Mozambican War of Independence</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sempreatentos...aoperigo!.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Sempreatentos...aoperigo%21.jpg/250px-Sempreatentos...aoperigo%21.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="214" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Sempreatentos...aoperigo%21.jpg/375px-Sempreatentos...aoperigo%21.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Sempreatentos...aoperigo%21.jpg/500px-Sempreatentos...aoperigo%21.jpg 2x" data-file-width="511" data-file-height="437" /></a><figcaption>Portuguese troops during the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Colonial_War" title="Portuguese Colonial War">Portuguese Colonial War</a>, some loading <a href="/wiki/FN_FAL" title="FN FAL">FN FAL</a> and G3</figcaption></figure>
<p>As <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communist</a> and anti-colonial ideologies spread out across Africa, many clandestine political movements were established in support of Mozambican independence. These movements claimed that since policies and development plans were primarily designed by the ruling authorities for the benefit of Mozambique's Portuguese population, little attention was paid to Mozambique's tribal integration and the development of its native communities.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the official guerrilla statements, this affected a majority of the indigenous population who suffered both state-sponsored discrimination and enormous social pressure. Many felt they had received too little opportunity or resources to upgrade their skills and improve their economic and social situation to a degree comparable to that of the Europeans. Statistically, Mozambique's Portuguese whites were indeed wealthier and more skilled than the black indigenous majority. As a response to the guerrilla movement, the Portuguese government from the 1960s and principally the early 1970s initiated gradual changes with new socioeconomic developments and egalitarian policies.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Front for the Liberation of Mozambique (<a href="/wiki/FRELIMO" title="FRELIMO">FRELIMO</a>) initiated a guerrilla campaign against Portuguese rule in September 1964. This conflict—along with the two others already initiated in the other Portuguese colonies of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Angola" title="Portuguese Angola">Angola</a> and <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Guinea" title="Portuguese Guinea">Portuguese Guinea</a>—became part of the so-called <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Colonial_War" title="Portuguese Colonial War">Portuguese Colonial War</a> (1961–1974). From a military standpoint, the Portuguese regular army maintained control of the population centres while the guerrilla forces sought to undermine their influence in rural and tribal areas in the north and west. As part of their response to FRELIMO, the Portuguese government began to pay more attention to creating favourable conditions for social development and economic growth.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Independence_.281975.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Independence_(1975)">Independence (1975)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Independence (1975)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>FRELIMO took control of the territory after ten years of sporadic warfare, as well as Portugal's own return to democracy after the fall of the authoritarian <a href="/wiki/Estado_Novo_(Portugal)" title="Estado Novo (Portugal)">Estado Novo</a> regime in the <a href="/wiki/Carnation_Revolution" title="Carnation Revolution">Carnation Revolution</a> of April 1974 and the failed <a href="/wiki/Coup_of_25_November_1975" title="Coup of 25 November 1975">coup of 25 November 1975</a>. Within a year, most of the 250,000 Portuguese in Mozambique had left—some expelled by the government of the nearly independent territory, some left the country to avoid possible reprisals from the unstable government—and Mozambique became independent from Portugal on 25 June 1975.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> A law had been passed on the initiative of the relatively unknown <a href="/wiki/Armando_Guebuza" title="Armando Guebuza">Armando Guebuza</a> of the FRELIMO party, ordering the Portuguese to leave the country in 24 hours with only 20 kilograms (44 pounds) of luggage. Unable to salvage any of their assets, most of them returned to Portugal penniless.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Mozambican_Civil_War_.281977.E2.80.931992.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mozambican_Civil_War_(1977–1992)">Mozambican Civil War (1977–1992)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Mozambican Civil War (1977–1992)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Mozambique" title="People&#39;s Republic of Mozambique">People's Republic of Mozambique</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mozambican_Civil_War" title="Mozambican Civil War">Mozambican Civil War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Land_mine_victim_2_(4364914733).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Land_mine_victim_2_%284364914733%29.jpg/220px-Land_mine_victim_2_%284364914733%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Land_mine_victim_2_%284364914733%29.jpg/330px-Land_mine_victim_2_%284364914733%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Land_mine_victim_2_%284364914733%29.jpg/440px-Land_mine_victim_2_%284364914733%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>A land mine victim in Mozambique</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Mozambique" title="People&#39;s Republic of Mozambique">new government</a> under President <a href="/wiki/Samora_Machel" title="Samora Machel">Samora Machel</a> established a <a href="/wiki/One-party_state" title="One-party state">one-party state</a> based on <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxist</a> principles. It received diplomatic and some military support from <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> and proceeded to crack down on opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Starting shortly after independence, the country was plagued from 1977 to 1992 by a long and violent civil war between the opposition forces of anti-communist Mozambican National Resistance (<a href="/wiki/RENAMO" title="RENAMO">RENAMO</a>) rebel militias and the FRELIMO regime. This conflict characterised the first decades of Mozambican independence, combined with sabotage from the neighbouring states of <a href="/wiki/Rhodesia" title="Rhodesia">Rhodesia</a> and South Africa, ineffective policies, failed central planning, and the resulting economic collapse. This period was also marked by the exodus of Portuguese nationals and Mozambicans of Portuguese heritage,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> a collapsed infrastructure, lack of investment in productive assets, and government nationalisation of privately owned industries, as well as widespread famine.
</p><p>During most of the civil war, the FRELIMO-formed central government was unable to exercise effective control outside urban areas, many of which were cut off from the capital.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> RENAMO-controlled areas included up to 50% of the rural areas in several provinces, and it is reported that health services of any kind were isolated from assistance for years in those areas. The problem worsened when the government cut back spending on health care.<sup id="cite_ref-Pfeiffer._J._2003_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pfeiffer._J._2003-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The war was marked by mass human rights violations from both sides of the conflict, with both RENAMO and FRELIMO contributing to the chaos through the use of terror and indiscriminate targeting of civilians.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The central government executed tens of thousands of people while trying to extend its control throughout the country and sent many people to "re-education camps" where thousands died.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:RhodesiaAllies1975.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/RhodesiaAllies1975.png/220px-RhodesiaAllies1975.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/RhodesiaAllies1975.png/330px-RhodesiaAllies1975.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/RhodesiaAllies1975.png/440px-RhodesiaAllies1975.png 2x" data-file-width="624" data-file-height="475" /></a><figcaption>The geopolitical situation in 1975; nations friendly to the FRELIMO are shown in orange.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the war, RENAMO proposed a peace agreement based on the secession of RENAMO-controlled northern and western territories as the independent <i>Republic of Rombesia</i>, but FRELIMO refused, insisting on the undivided sovereignty of the entire country. An estimated one million Mozambicans perished during the civil war, 1.7&#160;million took refuge in neighbouring states, and several million more were internally displaced.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The FRELIMO regime also gave shelter and support to South African (<a href="/wiki/African_National_Congress" title="African National Congress">African National Congress</a>) and Zimbabwean (<a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe_African_National_Union" title="Zimbabwe African National Union">Zimbabwe African National Union</a>) rebel movements, while the governments of Rhodesia and later Apartheid South Africa backed RENAMO in the civil war.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Between 300,000 and 600,000 people died of famine during the war.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 19 October 1986, Machel was on his way back from an international meeting in Zambia in the presidential <a href="/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-134" title="Tupolev Tu-134">Tupolev Tu-134</a> aircraft when the <a href="/wiki/1986_Mozambican_Tupolev_Tu-134_crash" title="1986 Mozambican Tupolev Tu-134 crash">plane crashed</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Lebombo_Mountains" title="Lebombo Mountains">Lebombo Mountains</a> near <a href="/wiki/Mbuzini" title="Mbuzini">Mbuzini</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Mpumalanga" title="Mpumalanga">Mpumalanga</a> region of apartheid-ruling South Africa. There were ten survivors, but President Machel and thirty-three others died, including ministers and officials of the Mozambique government. The United Nations' Soviet delegation issued a minority report contending that their expertise and experience had been undermined by the South Africans. Representatives of the Soviet Union advanced the theory that the plane had been intentionally diverted by a false <a href="/wiki/VHF_omnidirectional_range" title="VHF omnidirectional range">navigational beacon</a> signal, using a technology provided by military intelligence operatives of the South African government.<sup id="cite_ref-TRC_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TRC-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Machel's successor <a href="/wiki/Joaquim_Chissano" title="Joaquim Chissano">Joaquim Chissano</a> implemented sweeping changes in the country, starting reforms such as changing from Marxism to capitalism and began peace talks with RENAMO. The new constitution enacted in 1990 provided for a <a href="/wiki/Multi-party_system" title="Multi-party system">multi-party political system</a>, <a href="/wiki/Market_economy" title="Market economy">market-based economy</a>, and free elections. The civil war ended in October 1992 with the <a href="/wiki/Rome_General_Peace_Accords" title="Rome General Peace Accords">Rome General Peace Accords</a>, first brokered by the Christian Council of Mozambique (Council of Protestant Churches) and then taken over by <a href="/wiki/Community_of_Sant%27Egidio" title="Community of Sant&#39;Egidio">Community of Sant'Egidio</a>. Peace returned to Mozambique, under the supervision of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Operation_in_Mozambique" title="United Nations Operation in Mozambique">peacekeeping force of the United Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Democratic_era_.281993.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Democratic_era_(1993–present)">Democratic era (1993–present)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Democratic era (1993–present)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg/220px-Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg/330px-Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg/440px-Helicopter_over_flooded_Central_Mozambique.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="683" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Sikorsky_MH-53" title="Sikorsky MH-53">US helicopter</a> flying over the flooded <a href="/wiki/Limpopo_River" title="Limpopo River">Limpopo River</a> during the <a href="/wiki/2000_Mozambique_flood" title="2000 Mozambique flood">2000 Mozambique flood</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Mozambique held elections in 1994, which were accepted by most political parties as free and fair although still contested by many nationals and observers alike. FRELIMO won, under Joaquim Chissano, while RENAMO, led by <a href="/wiki/Afonso_Dhlakama" title="Afonso Dhlakama">Afonso Dhlakama</a>, ran as the official opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In 1995, Mozambique joined the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>, becoming, at the time, the only member nation that had never been part of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By mid-1995, over 1.7&#160;million refugees who had sought asylum in neighbouring countries had returned to Mozambique, part of the largest repatriation witnessed in sub-Saharan Africa. An additional four million <a href="/wiki/Internally_displaced_person" title="Internally displaced person">internally displaced persons</a> had returned to their homes.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In December 1999, Mozambique held elections for a second time since the civil war, which were again won by FRELIMO. RENAMO accused FRELIMO of fraud and threatened to return to civil war but backed down after taking the matter to the Supreme Court and losing.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early 2000, a cyclone caused <a href="/wiki/2000_Mozambique_flood" title="2000 Mozambique flood">widespread flooding</a>, killing hundreds and devastating the already precarious infrastructure.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> There were widespread suspicions that foreign aid resources had been diverted by powerful leaders of FRELIMO. <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Cardoso_(journalist)" title="Carlos Cardoso (journalist)">Carlos Cardoso</a>, a journalist investigating these allegations, was murdered,<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> and his death was never satisfactorily explained.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Indicating in 2001 that he would not run for a third term,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Chissano criticised leaders who stayed on longer than he had, which was generally seen as a reference to Zambian President <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Chiluba" title="Frederick Chiluba">Frederick Chiluba</a>, who at the time was considering a third term, and Zimbabwean President <a href="/wiki/Robert_Mugabe" title="Robert Mugabe">Robert Mugabe</a>, then in his fourth term.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Presidential and National Assembly elections took place on 1–2 December 2004. FRELIMO candidate <a href="/wiki/Armando_Guebuza" title="Armando Guebuza">Armando Guebuza</a> won<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> with 64% of the popular vote, and Dhlakama received 32% of the popular vote. FRELIMO won 160 seats in Parliament, with a coalition of RENAMO and several small parties winning the 90 remaining seats. Guebuza was inaugurated as the President of Mozambique on 2 February 2005<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and served two five-year terms. His successor, <a href="/wiki/Filipe_Nyusi" title="Filipe Nyusi">Filipe Nyusi</a>, became the fourth President of Mozambique on 15 January 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 2013 to 2019, a low<a href="/wiki/Intensity" class="mw-disambig" title="Intensity">-intensity</a> <a href="/wiki/RENAMO_insurgency_(2013%E2%80%932021)" title="RENAMO insurgency (2013–2021)">insurgency by RENAMO</a> occurred, mainly in the country's central and northern regions. On 5 September 2014, Guebuza and Dhlakama signed the Accord on Cessation of Hostilities, which brought the military hostilities to a halt and allowed both parties to concentrate on the general elections to be held in October 2014. However, after the general elections, a new political crisis emerged. RENAMO did not recognise the validity of the election results and demanded the control of six provinces – Nampula, Niassa, Tete, Zambezia, Sofala, and Manica – where they claimed to have won a majority.<sup id="cite_ref-:02_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:02-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> About 12,000 refugees fled to <a href="/wiki/Malawi" title="Malawi">Malawi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Refugees" title="United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees">UNHCR</a>, <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A9decins_Sans_Fronti%C3%A8res" title="Médecins Sans Frontières">Doctors Without Borders</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch" title="Human Rights Watch">Human Rights Watch</a> reported that government forces had torched villages and carried out <a href="/wiki/Summary_execution" title="Summary execution">summary executions</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wartime_sexual_violence" title="Wartime sexual violence">sexual abuses</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2019, President Filipe Nyusi was re-elected after a landslide victory in <a href="/wiki/2019_Mozambican_general_election" title="2019 Mozambican general election">general election</a>. FRELIMO won 184 seats, RENAMO got 60 seats and the MDM party received the remaining 6 seats in the National Assembly. Opposition did not accept the results because of allegations of fraud and irregularities. FRELIMO secured two-thirds majority in parliament which allowed FRELIMO to re-adjust the constitution without needing the agreement of the opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since 2017, the country has faced an <a href="/wiki/Insurgency_in_Cabo_Delgado" title="Insurgency in Cabo Delgado">ongoing insurgency by Islamist groups</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In September 2020, <a href="/wiki/Islamic_State" title="Islamic State">ISIL</a> insurgents captured and briefly occupied <a href="/wiki/Vamizi_Island" title="Vamizi Island">Vamizi Island</a> in the Indian Ocean.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> In March 2021, dozens of civilians were killed and 35,000 others were displaced after <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Palma" title="Battle of Palma">Islamist rebels seized</a> the city of <a href="/wiki/Palma,_Mozambique" title="Palma, Mozambique">Palma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> In December 2021, nearly 4,000 Mozambicans fled their villages after an intensification of jihadist attacks in <a href="/wiki/Niassa_Province" title="Niassa Province">Niassa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Mozambique" title="Geography of Mozambique">Geography of Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mozambique_sat.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Mozambique_sat.png/170px-Mozambique_sat.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Mozambique_sat.png/255px-Mozambique_sat.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Mozambique_sat.png/340px-Mozambique_sat.png 2x" data-file-width="1277" data-file-height="1968" /></a><figcaption>Satellite image</figcaption></figure>
<p>At 309,475&#160;sq&#160;mi (801,537&#160;km<sup>2</sup>), Mozambique is the world's 35th-largest country. Mozambique is located on the southeast coast of <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a> and is bound by <a href="/wiki/Eswatini" title="Eswatini">Eswatini</a> to the south, <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a> to the southwest, <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe">Zimbabwe</a> to the west, <a href="/wiki/Zambia" title="Zambia">Zambia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Malawi" title="Malawi">Malawi</a> to the northwest, <a href="/wiki/Tanzania" title="Tanzania">Tanzania</a> to the north and the Indian Ocean to the east. Mozambique lies between latitudes <a href="/wiki/10th_parallel_south" title="10th parallel south">10°</a> and <a href="/wiki/27th_parallel_south" title="27th parallel south">27°S</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/30th_meridian_east" title="30th meridian east">30°</a> and <a href="/wiki/41st_meridian_east" title="41st meridian east">41°E</a>.
</p><p>The country is divided into two topographical regions by the Zambezi River. To the north of the Zambezi, the narrow coastal strip gives way to inland hills and low plateaus. Rugged highlands are further west; they include the Niassa highlands, <a href="/wiki/Mount_Namuli" title="Mount Namuli">Namuli</a> or Shire highlands, Angonia highlands, Tete highlands and the Makonde plateau, covered with <a href="/wiki/Miombo" title="Miombo">miombo</a> woodlands. To the south of the Zambezi, the lowlands are broader with the Mashonaland plateau and <a href="/wiki/Lebombo_Mountains" title="Lebombo Mountains">Lebombo Mountains</a> located in the deep south.
</p><p>The country is drained by five principal rivers and several smaller ones with the largest and most important the Zambezi. The country has four notable lakes: <a href="/wiki/Lake_Malawi" title="Lake Malawi">Lake Niassa</a> (or Malawi), <a href="/wiki/Lake_Chiuta" title="Lake Chiuta">Lake Chiuta</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cahora_Bassa" title="Cahora Bassa">Cahora Bassa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lake_Shirwa" title="Lake Shirwa">Lake Shirwa</a>, all in the north. The major cities are <a href="/wiki/Maputo" title="Maputo">Maputo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Beira,_Mozambique" title="Beira, Mozambique">Beira</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nampula" title="Nampula">Nampula</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tete,_Mozambique" title="Tete, Mozambique">Tete</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quelimane" title="Quelimane">Quelimane</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chimoio" title="Chimoio">Chimoio</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pemba,_Mozambique" title="Pemba, Mozambique">Pemba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Inhambane" title="Inhambane">Inhambane</a>, <a href="/wiki/Xai-Xai" title="Xai-Xai">Xai-Xai</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lichinga" title="Lichinga">Lichinga</a>.
</p>
