<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Climate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Climate_of_Mozambique" class="mw-redirect" title="Climate of Mozambique">Climate of Mozambique</a> and <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Mozambique" title="Climate change in Mozambique">Climate change in Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg/290px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="204" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg/435px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg/580px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MOZ_present.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1208" data-file-height="850" /></a><figcaption>Mozambique map of Köppen climate classification zones</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mozambique has a tropical climate with two seasons: a <a href="/wiki/Wet_season" title="Wet season">wet season</a> from October to March and a <a href="/wiki/Dry_season" title="Dry season">dry season</a> from April to September. Climatic conditions, however, vary depending on altitude. Rainfall is heavy along the coast and decreases in the north and south. Annual precipitation varies from 500 to 900&#160;mm (19.7 to 35.4&#160;in) depending on the region, with an average of 590&#160;mm (23.2&#160;in). Cyclones are common during the wet season. Average temperature ranges in Maputo are from 13 to 24&#160;°C (55.4 to 75.2&#160;°F) in July and from 22 to 31&#160;°C (71.6 to 87.8&#160;°F) in February.
</p><p>In 2019 Mozambique suffered floods and destruction from the devastating cyclones <a href="/wiki/Cyclone_Idai" title="Cyclone Idai">Idai</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cyclone_Kenneth" title="Cyclone Kenneth">Kenneth</a>, the first time two cyclones had struck the nation in a single season.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thousands of crops were destroyed during the flooding, which causes transboundary animal diseases, and over 10 million people were affected throughout the region, according to the FAO's urgent campaign for southern Africa, which includes Malawi, Madagascar, and Mozambique. These countries have been experiencing climate disasters between January and March 2023 that have seriously affected various sectors, including farming, fisheries, and thousands of crops.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wildlife">Wildlife</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Wildlife">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Mozambique" title="Wildlife of Mozambique">Wildlife of Mozambique</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Category:Flora_of_Mozambique" title="Category:Flora of Mozambique">Category:Flora of Mozambique</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_marine_molluscs_of_Mozambique" title="List of marine molluscs of Mozambique">List of marine molluscs of Mozambique</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_non-marine_molluscs_of_Mozambique" title="List of non-marine molluscs of Mozambique">List of non-marine molluscs of Mozambique</a></div>
<p>There are known to be <a href="/wiki/List_of_birds_of_Mozambique" title="List of birds of Mozambique">740 bird species</a> in Mozambique, including 20 globally threatened species and two introduced species, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_mammals_of_Mozambique" title="List of mammals of Mozambique">over 200 mammal species</a> endemic to Mozambique, including the critically endangered <a href="/wiki/Selous%27_zebra" title="Selous&#39; zebra">Selous' zebra</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vincent%27s_bush_squirrel" title="Vincent&#39;s bush squirrel">Vincent's bush squirrel</a> and 13 other endangered or vulnerable species.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Mozambique" title="List of protected areas of Mozambique">Protected areas</a> include thirteen forest reserves, seven national parks, six nature reserves, three frontier conservation areas and three wildlife or game reserves. The country had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 6.93/10, ranking it 62nd globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Politics">Politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Mozambique" title="Politics of Mozambique">Politics of Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Filipe_Nyusi.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Filipe_Nyusi.jpg/220px-Filipe_Nyusi.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Filipe_Nyusi.jpg/330px-Filipe_Nyusi.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Filipe_Nyusi.jpg/440px-Filipe_Nyusi.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5472" data-file-height="3648" /></a><figcaption>President <a href="/wiki/Filipe_Nyusi" title="Filipe Nyusi">Filipe Nyusi</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg/220px-Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="137" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg/330px-Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg/440px-Conselho_Municipal_de_Maputo_frente.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1928" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Maputo City Hall</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Mozambique" title="Constitution of Mozambique">Constitution of Mozambique</a> stipulates that the <a href="/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_Mozambique" title="List of presidents of Mozambique">President of the Republic</a> functions as the head of state, head of government, commander-in-chief of the armed forces, and as a symbol of national unity.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> He is <a href="/wiki/Direct_election" title="Direct election">directly elected</a> for a five-year term via run-off voting; if no candidate receives more than half of the votes cast in the first round of voting, a second round of voting will be held in which only the two candidates who received the highest number of votes in the first round will participate, and whichever of the candidates obtains a majority of votes in the second round will thus be elected president. The <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Mozambique" title="List of prime ministers of Mozambique">prime minister</a> is appointed by the president. His functions include convening and chairing the council of ministers (cabinet), advising the president, assisting the president in governing the country, and coordinating the functions of the other ministers.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Assembly_of_the_Republic_of_Mozambique" class="mw-redirect" title="Assembly of the Republic of Mozambique">Assembly of the Republic</a> (<i>Assembleia da República</i>) has 250 members, elected for a five-year term by <a href="/wiki/Proportional_representation" title="Proportional representation">proportional representation</a>. The judiciary comprises a Supreme Court and provincial, district, and municipal courts.
</p><p>Mozambique operates a small, functioning military that handles all aspects of domestic national defence, the <a href="/wiki/Mozambique_Defence_Armed_Forces" title="Mozambique Defence Armed Forces">Mozambique Defence Armed Forces</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Foreign relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Mozambique" title="Foreign relations of Mozambique">Foreign relations of Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Embassy_of_Mozambique,_Washington,_D.C..jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Embassy_of_Mozambique%2C_Washington%2C_D.C..jpg/170px-Embassy_of_Mozambique%2C_Washington%2C_D.C..jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Embassy_of_Mozambique%2C_Washington%2C_D.C..jpg/255px-Embassy_of_Mozambique%2C_Washington%2C_D.C..jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Embassy_of_Mozambique%2C_Washington%2C_D.C..jpg/340px-Embassy_of_Mozambique%2C_Washington%2C_D.C..jpg 2x" data-file-width="1625" data-file-height="1843" /></a><figcaption>Mozambique's embassy in Washington, D.C.</figcaption></figure>
<p>While allegiances dating back to the liberation struggle remain relevant, Mozambique's foreign policy has become increasingly pragmatic. The twin pillars of Mozambique's foreign policy are maintenance of good relations with its neighbours<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> and maintenance and expansion of ties to development partners.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the 1970s and the early 1980s, Mozambique's foreign policy was inextricably linked to the struggles for majority rule in Rhodesia and South Africa as well as superpower competition and the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Mozambique's decision to enforce UN sanctions against Rhodesia and deny that country access to the sea led <a href="/wiki/Ian_Smith" title="Ian Smith">Ian Smith</a>'s government to undertake overt and covert actions to oppose the country. Although the change of government in Zimbabwe in 1980 removed this threat, the government of South Africa continued to destabilise Mozambique.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Mozambique also belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Frontline_States" title="Frontline States">Frontline States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The 1984 <a href="/wiki/Nkomati_Accord" title="Nkomati Accord">Nkomati Accord</a>, while failing in its goal of ending South African support to RENAMO, opened initial diplomatic contacts between the Mozambican and South African governments. This process gained momentum with South Africa's elimination of <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a>, which culminated in the establishment of full diplomatic relations in October 1993. While relations with neighbouring Zimbabwe, Malawi, Zambia and Tanzania show occasional strains, Mozambique's ties to these countries remain strong.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the years immediately following its independence, Mozambique benefited from considerable assistance from some Western countries, notably the Scandinavians. The Soviet Union and its allies became Mozambique's primary economic, military and political supporters, and its foreign policy reflected this linkage. This began to change in 1983; in 1984 Mozambique joined the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> and <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a>. Western aid by the Scandinavian countries of Sweden, <a href="/wiki/Norway" title="Norway">Norway</a>, <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iceland" title="Iceland">Iceland</a> quickly replaced Soviet support.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Finland" title="Finland">Finland</a><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a> are becoming increasingly important sources of development assistance. Italy also maintains a profile in Mozambique as a result of its key role during the peace process. Relations with Portugal, the former colonial power, continue to be important because Portuguese investors play a visible role in Mozambique's economy.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg/220px-Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg/330px-Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg/440px-Prime_Minister_Narendra_Modi_meets_members_of_Indian_Community_in_Mozambique.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2200" data-file-height="1307" /></a><figcaption>Indian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Narendra_Modi" title="Narendra Modi">Narendra Modi</a> meets members of <a href="/wiki/Indians_in_Mozambique" title="Indians in Mozambique">Indian community in Mozambique</a>, 7 July 2016.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mozambique is a member of the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a> and ranks among the moderate members of the African bloc in the United Nations and other international organisations. Mozambique also belongs to the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community" title="Southern African Development Community">Southern African Development Community</a>. In 1994, the government became a full member of the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of the Islamic Conference</a>, in part to broaden its base of international support but also to please the country's sizeable Muslim population. Similarly, in 1995 Mozambique joined its Anglophone neighbours in the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>. At the time it was the only nation to have joined the Commonwealth that was never part of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>. In the same year, Mozambique became a founding member and the first President of the <a href="/wiki/Community_of_Portuguese_Language_Countries" title="Community of Portuguese Language Countries">Community of Portuguese Language Countries</a> and maintains close ties with other Portuguese-speaking countries.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Human_rights">Human rights</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Human rights">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Mozambique" title="Human rights in Mozambique">Human rights in Mozambique</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Human_trafficking_in_Mozambique" title="Human trafficking in Mozambique">Human trafficking in Mozambique</a></div>
<p>Same-sex sexual activity has been legal since 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> However, discrimination against <a href="/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Mozambique" title="LGBT rights in Mozambique">LGBT people</a> in Mozambique is widespread.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Administrative divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_Mozambique" title="Provinces of Mozambique">Provinces of Mozambique</a>, <a href="/wiki/Districts_of_Mozambique" title="Districts of Mozambique">Districts of Mozambique</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Postos_of_Mozambique" title="Postos of Mozambique">Postos of Mozambique</a></div>
<p>Mozambique is divided into ten provinces (<i>provincias</i>) and one capital city (<i>cidade capital</i>) with provincial status. The provinces are subdivided into 129 districts (<i>distritos</i>). The districts are further divided into 405 "<i>postos administrativos</i>" (administrative posts, headed by <i>secretários</i>) and then into <i>localidades</i> (localities), the lowest geographical level of the central state administration. There are 53 "<i>municípios</i>" (municipalities).
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg/220px-GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg/330px-GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg/440px-GDP_per_capita_development_in_Mozambique.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>Historical development of real GDP per capita in Mozambique, since 1960</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mozambique_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Mozambique_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/350px-Mozambique_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="228" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Mozambique_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/525px-Mozambique_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Mozambique_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/700px-Mozambique_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a><figcaption>A proportional representation of Mozambique's exports</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mozambique is one of the poorest and most underdeveloped countries in the world, even though between 1994 and 2006 its average annual GDP growth was approximately 8%. The <a href="/wiki/IMF" class="mw-redirect" title="IMF">IMF</a> classifies Mozambique as a <a href="/wiki/Heavily_indebted_poor_countries" title="Heavily indebted poor countries">heavily indebted poor country</a>. In a 2006 survey, three-quarters of Mozambicans said that in the past five years their economic position had remained the same or become worse.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mozambique's official currency is the <a href="/wiki/Mozambican_metical" title="Mozambican metical">metical</a> (as of December 2022, US$1 is roughly equivalent to 64 meticals) The <a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">U.S. dollar</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_African_rand" title="South African rand">South African rand</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Euro" title="Euro">euro</a> are widely accepted and used in business transactions. The minimum legal salary is around US$60 per month. Mozambique is a member of the <a href="/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community" title="Southern African Development Community">Southern African Development Community</a> (SADC).<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The SADC <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> protocol is aimed at making the Southern African region more competitive by eliminating <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> and other <a href="/wiki/Trade_barrier" title="Trade barrier">trade barriers</a>. The <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> in 2007 talked of Mozambique's 'blistering pace of economic growth'. A joint donor-government study in early 2007 said 'Mozambique is generally considered an aid success story.'
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rebounding_growth">Rebounding growth</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Rebounding growth">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The resettlement of civil war refugees and successful economic reform have led to a high growth rate: the country enjoyed a remarkable recovery, achieving an average annual rate of economic growth of 8% between 1996 and 2006<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> and between 6–7% from 2006 to 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-databank.worldbank.org_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-databank.worldbank.org-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Rapid expansion in the future hinges on several major foreign investment projects, continued economic reform, and the revival of the agriculture, transportation, and tourism sectors.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In 2013 about 80% of the population was employed in agriculture, the majority of whom were engaged in small-scale subsistence farming<sup id="cite_ref-Canada_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Canada-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> which still suffered from inadequate infrastructure, commercial networks, and investment.<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> However, in 2012, more than 90% of Mozambique's arable land was still <a href="/wiki/Tillage" title="Tillage">uncultivated</a>.
</p><p>In 2013, a BBC article reported that starting in 2009, the Portuguese had been returning to Mozambique because of the growing economy in Mozambique and the poor economic situation in Portugal.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_reforms">Economic reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Economic reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>More than 1,200 mostly small <a href="/wiki/Nationalization" title="Nationalization">state-owned enterprises</a> have been <a href="/wiki/Privatization" title="Privatization">privatised</a>. Preparations for privatisation and/or sector liberalisation were made for the remaining parastatal enterprises, including telecommunications, energy, ports, and railways. The government frequently selected a strategic foreign investor when privatising a parastatal. Additionally, customs duties have been reduced, and customs management has been streamlined and reformed. The government introduced a value-added tax in 1999 as part of its efforts to increase domestic revenues.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Corruption">Corruption</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Corruption">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg/220px-Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg/330px-Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg/440px-Mozambique_-_traditional_sailboat.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1089" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption>Traditional sailboat in <a href="/wiki/Ilha_de_Mo%C3%A7ambique" class="mw-redirect" title="Ilha de Moçambique">Ilha de Moçambique</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Mozambique's economy has been shaken by numerous corruption scandals. In July 2011, the government proposed new anti-corruption laws to criminalise embezzlement, influence peddling and <a href="/wiki/Graft_(politics)" title="Graft (politics)">graft</a>, following numerous instances of the theft of public money. This has been endorsed by the country's Council of Ministers. Mozambique convicted two former ministers for graft.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Mozambique was ranked 116 of 178 countries in anti-graft watchdog <a href="/wiki/Transparency_International" title="Transparency International">Transparency International</a>'s index of global corruption. According to a USAID report written in 2005, "the scale and scope of corruption in Mozambique are cause for alarm."<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2012, the government of Inhambane province uncovered the misappropriation of public funds by the director of the Provincial Anti-Drugs Office, Calisto Alberto Tomo. He was found to have colluded with the accountant in the Anti-Drugs Office, Recalda Guambe, to steal over 260,000 meticais between 2008 and 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The government of Mozambique has taken steps to address the problem of corruption, and some positive developments can be observed, such as the passages of several anti-corruption bills in 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Natural_resources">Natural resources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Natural resources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mineral_industry_of_Mozambique" title="Mineral industry of Mozambique">Mineral industry of Mozambique</a></div>
<p>In 2010–2011, <a href="/wiki/Anadarko_Petroleum" title="Anadarko Petroleum">Anadarko Petroleum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eni" title="Eni">Eni</a> discovered the <a href="/wiki/Mamba_South_gas_field" title="Mamba South gas field">Mamba South gas field</a>, recoverable reserves of 4,200&#160;billion cubic metres (150&#160;trillion cubic feet) of natural gas in the <a href="/wiki/Ruvuma_River" title="Ruvuma River">Rovuma</a> Basin, off the coast of northern Cabo Delgado Province. Once developed, this could make Mozambique one of the largest producers of <a href="/wiki/Liquefied_natural_gas" title="Liquefied natural gas">liquefied natural gas</a> in the world. In January 2017, 3 firms were selected by the government for the natural gas development projects in the Rovuma gas basin. GL Africa Energy (UK) was awarded one of the tenders. It plans to build and operate a 250 MW gas-powered plant.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Production was scheduled to start in 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Mozambique is now scheduled to begin exporting LNG globally in 2024. In 2019, developments in the Rovuma Basin, referred to as The Mozambique LNG Project, raised $19&#160;billion from a consortium of investors to finally bring this LNG to market. The majority of the project and its associated operations have been awarded to the company, TotalEnergies.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tourism">Tourism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Tourism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_Mozambique" title="Tourism in Mozambique">Tourism in Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg/220px-La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg/330px-La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg/440px-La_plage_aux_dunes_de_Dovela_-_2012-04-28_-_75154574.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="427" /></a><figcaption>European tourists on the beach, in <a href="/wiki/Inhambane_Province" title="Inhambane Province">Inhambane</a>, Mozambique</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg/220px-Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg/330px-Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg/440px-Vilaculos_beach_Mozambique_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6000" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>Vilanculos beach Mozambique</figcaption></figure>
<p>The country's natural environment, wildlife, and historic heritage provide opportunities for beach, cultural, and <a href="/wiki/Eco-tourism" class="mw-redirect" title="Eco-tourism">eco-tourism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Mozambique has a great potential for growth in its gross domestic product (GDP).<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg/220px-Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg/330px-Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg/440px-Carrying_goods_on_head_in_Mozambique.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6000" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>Carrying goods on head in Mozambique</figcaption></figure>
<p>The north beaches with clean water are suitable for tourism,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:No_original_research" title="Wikipedia:No original research"><span title="The material near this tag possibly contains original research. (July 2018)">original research?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> especially those that are very far from urban centres, such the Quirimbas Islands and the archipelago of Bazaruto.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The Inhambane Province attracts international divers because of the marine biodiversity and the presence of <a href="/wiki/Whale_shark" title="Whale shark">whale sharks</a> and <a href="/wiki/Manta_ray" title="Manta ray">manta rays</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> There are several national parks, including <a href="/wiki/Gorongosa_National_Park" title="Gorongosa National Park">Gorongosa National Park</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Transport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Mozambique" title="Transport in Mozambique">Transport in Mozambique</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Mozambique" class="mw-redirect" title="Rail transport in Mozambique">Rail transport in Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg/220px-Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg/330px-Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg/440px-Steam_locomotive_Inhambane.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2929" data-file-height="1947" /></a><figcaption>Steam locomotive at Inhambane, 2009</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png/220px-LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png/330px-LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png/440px-LAM_Boeing_737-200Adv_C9-BAK_JNB_2005-12-2.png 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="682" /></a><figcaption>National Mozambican airline, <a href="/wiki/LAM_Mozambique_Airlines" title="LAM Mozambique Airlines">LAM Mozambique</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>There are over 30,000&#160;km (19,000&#160;mi) of roads, but much of the network is unpaved. Like its <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a> neighbours, <a href="/wiki/Left-_and_right-hand_traffic" title="Left- and right-hand traffic">traffic circulates on the left</a>. There is an international airport at Maputo, 21 other paved airports, and over 100 airstrips with unpaved runways. There are 3,750&#160;km of <a href="/wiki/Navigability" title="Navigability">navigable</a> inland waterways. There are rail links serving principal cities and connecting the country with Malawi, Zimbabwe and South Africa. The Mozambican railway system developed over more than a century from three different ports on the coast that served as terminals for separate lines to the hinterland. The railroads were major targets during the Mozambican Civil War, were sabotaged by RENAMO, and are being rehabilitated. A <a href="/wiki/State-owned_enterprise" title="State-owned enterprise">parastatal</a> authority, <i>Portos e Caminhos de Ferro de Moçambique</i> (Mozambique Ports and Railways), oversees the railway system and its connected ports, but management has been largely outsourced. Each line has its own development corridor.
</p><p>As of 2005<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> there were 3,123&#160;km of railway track, consisting of 2,983&#160;km of <a href="/wiki/3_ft_6_in_gauge_railways" title="3 ft 6 in gauge railways"><span class="nowrap">1,067&#160;mm</span></a> (<span class="nowrap">3&#160;ft&#160;6&#160;in</span>) gauge, compatible with neighbouring rail systems, and a 140&#160;km line of <a href="/wiki/2_ft_6_in_gauge_railways" title="2 ft 6 in gauge railways"><span class="nowrap">762&#160;mm</span></a> (<span class="nowrap">2&#160;ft&#160;6&#160;in</span>) gauge, the <a href="/wiki/Gaza_Railway" title="Gaza Railway">Gaza Railway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The central <a href="/wiki/Beira%E2%80%93Bulawayo_railway" title="Beira–Bulawayo railway">Beira–Bulawayo railway</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sena_railway" title="Sena railway">Sena railway</a> route links the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_Beira" title="Port of Beira">port of Beira</a> to the landlocked countries of Malawi, Zambia and Zimbabwe. To the north of this the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_Nacala" title="Port of Nacala">port of Nacala</a> is also linked by <a href="/wiki/Nacala_railway" title="Nacala railway">Nacala rail</a> to Malawi, and to the south the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_Maputo" title="Port of Maputo">port of Maputo</a> is connected by the <a href="/wiki/Limpopo_railway" title="Limpopo railway">Limpopo rail</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Goba_railway" title="Goba railway">Goba rail</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Pretoria%E2%80%93Maputo_railway" title="Pretoria–Maputo railway">Ressano Garcia rail</a> to Zimbabwe, Eswatini and South Africa. These networks interconnect only via neighbouring countries. A new route for coal haulage between Tete and Beira was planned to come into service by 2010,<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> and in August 2010, Mozambique and <a href="/wiki/Botswana" title="Botswana">Botswana</a> signed a memorandum of understanding to develop a 1,100&#160;km railway through Zimbabwe, to carry coal from <a href="/wiki/Serule" title="Serule">Serule</a> in Botswana to a deepwater port at <a href="/wiki/Techobanine" title="Techobanine">Techobanine</a> Point.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Newer rolling stock has been supplied by the Indian <a href="/wiki/Golden_Rock_Railway_Workshop" title="Golden Rock Railway Workshop">Golden Rock</a> workshop<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> using <a href="/wiki/Janney_coupler" title="Janney coupler">Centre Buffer Couplers</a><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Railway_air_brake" title="Railway air brake">air brakes</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Water_supply_and_sanitation">Water supply and sanitation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Water supply and sanitation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Daily_struggle_for_water_(5400691444).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Daily_struggle_for_water_%285400691444%29.jpg/220px-Daily_struggle_for_water_%285400691444%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Daily_struggle_for_water_%285400691444%29.jpg/330px-Daily_struggle_for_water_%285400691444%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Daily_struggle_for_water_%285400691444%29.jpg/440px-Daily_struggle_for_water_%285400691444%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>Woman fetching water during the dry season from a polluted source in Machaze District of the Central Manica Province</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Mozambique" title="Water supply and sanitation in Mozambique">Water supply and sanitation in Mozambique</a> is characterised by low levels of access to an <a href="/wiki/Improved_water_source" title="Improved water source">improved water source</a> (estimated to be 51% in 2011), low levels of access to adequate sanitation (estimated to be 25% in 2011) and mostly poor service quality. In 2007 the government defined a strategy for water supply and sanitation in rural areas, where 62% of the population lives. In urban areas, water is supplied by informal small-scale providers and by formal providers.
</p><p>Beginning in 1998, Mozambique reformed the formal part of the urban water supply sector through the creation of an independent regulatory agency called CRA, an asset-holding company called FIPAG and a <a href="/wiki/Public-private_partnership" class="mw-redirect" title="Public-private partnership">public-private partnership</a> (PPP) with a company called Aguas de Moçambique.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The PPP covered those areas of the capital and of four other cities that had access to formal water supply systems. However, the PPP ended when the management contracts for four cities expired in 2008 and when the foreign partner of the company that serves the capital under a lease contract withdrew in 2010, claiming heavy losses. While urban water supply has received considerable policy attention, the government has no strategy for urban sanitation yet. External donors finance about 87.4% of all public investments in the sector.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Mozambique" title="Demographics of Mozambique">Demographics of Mozambique</a></div>
<table class="wikitable" style="float: right; margin-left: 10px">

<p>The north-central provinces of Zambezia and Nampula are the most populous, with about 45% of the population. The estimated four million <a href="/wiki/Makua_people" title="Makua people">Makua</a> are the dominant group in the northern part of the country; the <a href="/wiki/Sena_people" title="Sena people">Sena</a> and <a href="/wiki/Shona_people" title="Shona people">Shona</a> (mostly <a href="/wiki/Ndau_people" title="Ndau people">Ndau</a>) are prominent in the Zambezi valley,<sup id="cite_ref-BilateralRelationsFactSheet_19-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BilateralRelationsFactSheet-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Tsonga_people" title="Tsonga people">Tsonga</a> and <a href="/wiki/Soshangane" title="Soshangane">Shangaan</a> people dominate southern Mozambique. Other groups include <a href="/wiki/Makonde_people" title="Makonde people">Makonde</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yao_people_(East_Africa)" title="Yao people (East Africa)">Yao</a>, <a href="/wiki/Swahili_people" title="Swahili people">Swahili</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tonga_people_(Zambia_and_Zimbabwe)" title="Tonga people (Zambia and Zimbabwe)">Tonga</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chopi_people" title="Chopi people">Chopi</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Nguni_people" title="Nguni people">Nguni</a> (including <a href="/wiki/Zulu_people" title="Zulu people">Zulu</a>). <a href="/wiki/Bantu_peoples" title="Bantu peoples">Bantu</a> people comprise 97.8% of the population, with the rest made up of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Mozambicans" title="Portuguese Mozambicans">Portuguese</a> ancestry, Euro-Africans (<a href="/wiki/Creole_peoples#Africa" title="Creole peoples"><i>mestiço</i></a> people of mixed Bantu and Portuguese ancestry), and Indians.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Roughly 45,000 people of <a href="/wiki/Indians_in_Mozambique" title="Indians in Mozambique">Indian descent</a> reside in Mozambique.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During Portuguese colonial rule, a large minority of people of Portuguese descent lived permanently in almost all areas of the country,<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> and Mozambicans with <a href="/wiki/White_Africans_of_European_ancestry#Portuguese_in_Africa" title="White Africans of European ancestry">Portuguese heritage</a> at the time of independence numbered about 360,000.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Many of these left the country after independence from Portugal in 1975.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> There are various estimates for the size of <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_Chinese_in_Mozambique" title="Ethnic Chinese in Mozambique">Mozambique's Chinese community</a>, ranging from 7,000 to 12,000 as of 2007<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ISN_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ISN-115">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to a 2011 survey, the total fertility rate was 5.9 children per woman, with 6.6 in rural areas and 4.5 in urban areas.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Mozambique" title="Languages of Mozambique">Languages of Mozambique</a></div>

<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mz_etnies.PNG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Mz_etnies.PNG/290px-Mz_etnies.PNG" decoding="async" width="290" height="392" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Mz_etnies.PNG/435px-Mz_etnies.PNG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Mz_etnies.PNG/580px-Mz_etnies.PNG 2x" data-file-width="739" data-file-height="1000" /></a><figcaption>Ethnic map of Mozambique</figcaption></figure>
<p>Portuguese is the official and most widely spoken language of the nation, spoken by 50.3% of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
The Bantu-group languages that are indigenous to the country vary greatly in their groupings and in some cases are rather poorly appreciated and documented.<sup id="cite_ref-r1_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-r1-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Apart from its lingua franca uses in the north of the country, <a href="/wiki/Swahili_language" title="Swahili language">Swahili</a> is spoken in a small area of the coast next to the Tanzanian border; south of this, towards Moçambique Island, <a href="/wiki/Mwani_language" title="Mwani language">Kimwani</a>, regarded as a dialect of Swahili, is used. Immediately inland of the Swahili area, <a href="/wiki/Makonde_language" title="Makonde language">Makonde</a> is used, separated farther inland by a small strip of <a href="/wiki/Makhuwa_language" title="Makhuwa language">Makhuwa</a>-speaking territory from an area where <a href="/wiki/Yao_language" title="Yao language">Yao</a> or ChiYao is used. Makonde and Yao belong to a different group, Yao<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> being very close to the <a href="/wiki/Mwera_language" title="Mwera language">Mwera language</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Rondo_Plateau" title="Rondo Plateau">Rondo Plateau</a> area in Tanzania.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Prepositions appear in these languages as locative prefixes prefixed to the noun and declined according to their own noun-class. Some <a href="/wiki/Chewa_language" title="Chewa language">Nyanja</a> is used at the coast of Lake Malawi, as well as on the other side of the Lake.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Somewhat different from all of these are the languages of the eMakhuwa group, with a loss of initial k-, which means that many nouns begin with a vowel: for example, <i>epula</i> = "rain".<sup id="cite_ref-r1_120-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-r1-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> There is eMakhuwa proper, with the related <a href="/wiki/Lomwe_language" title="Lomwe language">eLomwe</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chuwabu_language" title="Chuwabu language">eChuwabo</a>, with a small <a href="/wiki/Koti_language" title="Koti language">eKoti</a>-speaking area at the coast. In an area straddling the lower Zambezi, <a href="/wiki/Sena_language" title="Sena language">Sena</a>, which belongs to the same group as Nyanja, is spoken, with areas speaking the related CiNyungwe and CiSenga further upriver.
</p><p>A large <a href="/wiki/Shona_language" title="Shona language">Shona</a>-speaking area extends between the Zimbabwe border and the sea: this was formerly known as the Ndau variety<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> but now uses the orthography of the Standard Shona of Zimbabwe. Apparently similar to Shona, but lacking the tone patterns of the Shona language, and regarded by its speakers as quite separate, is CiBalke, also called Rue or Barwe, used in a small area near the Zimbabwe border. South of this area are languages of the <a href="/wiki/Tsonga_language" title="Tsonga language">Tsonga</a> group. XiTswa or <a href="/wiki/Tswa_language" title="Tswa language">Tswa</a> occurs at the coast and inland, XiTsonga or Tsonga straddles the area around the Limpopo River, including such local dialects as XiHlanganu, XiN'walungu, XiBila, XiHlengwe, and XiDzonga. This language area extends into neighbouring South Africa. Still related to these, but distinct, are GiTonga, BiTonga, and CiCopi or <a href="/wiki/Chopi_language" title="Chopi language">Chopi</a>, spoken north of the mouth of the Limpopo, and XiRonga or <a href="/wiki/Ronga_language" title="Ronga language">Ronga</a>, spoken in the immediate region around Maputo. The languages in this group are, judging by the short vocabularies,<sup id="cite_ref-r1_120-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-r1-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> very vaguely similar to Zulu, but obviously not in the same immediate group. There are small Swazi- and Zulu-speaking areas in Mozambique immediately next to the Swaziland and KwaZulu-Natal borders.
</p><p>Arabs, Chinese, and Indians primarily speak Portuguese and some Hindi. Indians from Portuguese India speak any of the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese-based_creole_languages" title="Portuguese-based creole languages">Portuguese creoles</a> of their origin aside from Portuguese as their second language.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Mozambique" title="Religion in Mozambique">Religion in Mozambique</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" Immaculate Conception, Maputo</a></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:192px;max-width:192px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Mesquita_Maputo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Mesquita_Maputo.jpg/190px-Mesquita_Maputo.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="143" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Mesquita_Maputo.jpg/285px-Mesquita_Maputo.jpg 1.5x, mosque in downtown Maputo</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>The 2007 census found that Christians made up 59.2% of Mozambique's population, Muslims comprised 18.9% of the population, 7.3% of the people held other beliefs, mainly <a href="/wiki/Animism" title="Animism">animism</a>, and 13.9% had no religious beliefs.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_15-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> A more recent government survey conducted by the <a href="/wiki/Demographic_and_Health_Surveys" title="Demographic and Health Surveys">Demographic and Health Surveys</a> program in 2015 indicated that Catholicism had increased to 30.5% of the population, Muslims constituted 19.3%, and various Protestant groups a total of 44%.<sup id="cite_ref-DHS_2015_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DHS_2015-127">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> According to 2018 estimates from the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Commission_on_International_Religious_Freedom" title="United States Commission on International Religious Freedom">United States Commission on International Religious Freedom</a>, 28% of the population is Catholic, 18% are Muslim (mostly Sunni), 15% are <a href="/wiki/Zionist_churches" title="Zionist churches">Zionist Christians</a>, 12% are Protestants, 7% are members of other religious groups, and 18% have no religion.<sup id="cite_ref-IRFR_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IRFR-128">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Catholic Church has established twelve dioceses (Beira, Chimoio, Gurué, Inhambane, Lichinga, Maputo, Nacala, Nampula, Pemba, Quelimane, Tete,<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> and Xai-Xai; archdioceses are <a href="/wiki/Beira,_Mozambique" title="Beira, Mozambique">Beira</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maputo" title="Maputo">Maputo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nampula" title="Nampula">Nampula</a>). Statistics for the dioceses range from a low 5.8% Catholics in the population in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Chimoio" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Chimoio">Diocese of Chimoio</a>, to 32.50% in Quelimane diocese (Anuario catolico de Mocambique). Among the main Protestant denominations are <a href="/wiki/United_Baptist_Church_of_Mozambique" title="United Baptist Church of Mozambique">Igreja União Baptista de Moçambique</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Assembleias_de_Deus" title="Assembleias de Deus">Assembleias de Deus</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist_Church" title="Seventh-day Adventist Church">Seventh-day Adventists</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Church_of_Southern_Africa" title="Anglican Church of Southern Africa">Anglican Church of Southern Africa</a>, the Igreja do Evangelho Completo de Deus, the <a href="/wiki/Igreja_Metodista_Unida" title="Igreja Metodista Unida">Igreja Metodista Unida</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Igreja_Presbiteriana_de_Mo%C3%A7ambique" title="Igreja Presbiteriana de Moçambique">Igreja Presbiteriana de Moçambique</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Igrejas_de_Cristo" title="Igrejas de Cristo">Igrejas de Cristo</a> and the Assembleia Evangélica de Deus. The work of Methodism in Mozambique started in 1890. Erwin Richards began a Methodist mission at Chicuque in Inhambane Province. The Igreja Metodista Unida em Moçambique (United Methodist Church in Mozambique) observed the 100th anniversary of Methodist presence in Mozambique in 1990. President Chissano praised the work and role of the UMC to more than 10,000 people who attended the ceremony. The United Methodist Church has tripled in size in Mozambique since 1998. There are more than 150,000 members in more than 180 congregations of the 24 districts. New pastors are ordained each year. New churches are chartered each year in each Annual Conference (north and south).<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> has established a growing presence. It first began sending missionaries to Mozambique in 1999, and, as of April 2015, has more than 7,943 members.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a> has been present in Mozambique since the early 1950s but did not openly identify itself in those years because of the strong influence of the Catholic Church which did not recognise it officially as a world religion. The independence in 1975 saw the entrance of new pioneers. In total, there are about 3,000 declared Baháʼís as of 2010<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>. Muslims are particularly present in the north of the country. They are organised in several "<a href="/wiki/Tariqa" title="Tariqa">tariqa</a>" or <a href="/wiki/Fraternity" title="Fraternity">brotherhoods</a>. Two national organisations also exist—the <i>Conselho Islâmico de Moçambique</i> and the <i>Congresso Islâmico de Moçambique</i>. There are also important Pakistani, Indian associations as well as some Shia communities. There is a very small but thriving Jewish community in Maputo.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Health">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_Mozambique" title="Health in Mozambique">Health in Mozambique</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png/290px-Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="202" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png/435px-Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png/580px-Bev%C3%B6lkerungspyramide_Mosambik_2016.png 2x" data-file-width="610" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Population pyramid 2016</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg/290px-Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg" decoding="async" width="290" height="177" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg/435px-Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg/580px-Number_of_patients_on_Anti_Retroviral_Treatment_in_Mozambique_2003-2011.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1072" data-file-height="653" /></a><figcaption>The increase in the number of HIV positive Mozambicans on <a href="/wiki/Antiretroviral_drug" class="mw-redirect" title="Antiretroviral drug">Antiretroviral treatment</a>, 2003–14</figcaption></figure>
<p>The fertility rate is at about 5.5 births per woman. Public expenditure on health was at 2.7% of the GDP in 2004, whereas private expenditure on health was at 1.3% in the same year. Health expenditure per capita was 42 US$ (PPP) in 2004. In the early 21st century there were 3 physicians per 100,000 people in the country. <a href="/wiki/Infant_mortality" title="Infant mortality">Infant mortality</a> was at 100 per 1,000 births in 2005.<sup id="cite_ref-hdrstats.undp.org_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hdrstats.undp.org-133">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> The 2010 maternal mortality rate per 100,000 births for Mozambique is 550. This is compared with 598.8 in 2008 and 385 in 1990. The under 5 mortality rate, per 1,000 births is 147 and the neonatal mortality as a percentage of under 5s mortality is 29. In Mozambique the number of midwives per 1,000 live births is 3 and the lifetime risk of death for pregnant women 1 in 37.<sup id="cite_ref-SOWMY_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SOWMY-134">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The official <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_HIV/AIDS_adult_prevalence_rate" title="List of countries by HIV/AIDS adult prevalence rate">HIV prevalence</a> in 2011 was 11.5% of the population aged between 15 and 49 years. In the southern parts of Mozambique—Maputo and Gaza provinces as well as the city of Maputo—the official figures are more than twice as high as the national average. In 2011 the health authorities estimated about 1.7&#160;million Mozambicans were HIV-positive, of whom 600,000 were in need of anti-retroviral treatment. As of December 2011, 240,000 were receiving such treatment, increasing to 416,000 in March 2014 according to the health authorities.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mozambique&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Mozambique" title="Education in Mozambique">Education in Mozambique</a></div>
<p>Portuguese is the primary language of instruction in all Mozambican schools. All Mozambicans are required by law to attend school through the primary level; however, a lot of children do not go to primary school because they have to work for their families' subsistence farms for a living. In 2007, one million children still did not go to school, most of them from poor rural families, and almost half of all teachers were unqualified. Girls enrollment increased from 3 million in 2002 to 4.1&#160;million in 2006 while the completion rate increased from 31,000 to 90,000, which testified a very poor completion rate.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After grade 7, pupils must take standardised national exams to enter secondary school, which runs from eighth to 10th grade.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Space in Mozambican universities is extremely limited; thus most pupils who complete pre-university school do not immediately proceed on to university studies. Many go to work as teachers or are unemployed. There are also institutes that give more vocational training, specialising in agricultural, technical or pedagogical studies, which students may attend after grade 10 in lieu of a pre-university school. After independence from Portugal in 1975, a number of Mozambican pupils continued to be admitted every year at Portuguese high schools, polytechnical institutes and universities, through bilateral agreements between the Portuguese government and the Mozambican government.
</p><p>According to 2010 estimates, the <a href="/wiki/Literacy_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Literacy rate">literacy rate</a> was 56.1% (70.8% male and 42.8% female).<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> By 2015, this had increased to 58.8% (73.3% male and 45.4% female).<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
